59,2026-08-21,"After getting a layoff notice in May, engineer Todd Hendricks, 46, started his job search. One Friday, he signed on to Zoom for the coding round of a multistep interview process and waited for someone to appear. No one did. 
He reached out to the recruiter and heard nothing back. The next Monday, an email landed in his inbox. The company said it was no longer moving forward with his application, based on his performance in the interview he says never happened. 
Hendricks is one of many American job seekers who believe the hiring process is “broken.” Finding a job has rarely been easy, but in 2026, fierce competition, the growing use of artificial intelligence and a lack of trust in employers have further complicated the process.
U.S. employers added a historically low number of jobs in 2025. Hiring bounced back in the spring but has cooled in recent months. Employers shed 23,000 roles in July. People are still getting hired, though much of the recent job growth has been concentrated in a few select sectors, like health care. The search is tougher for candidates like Hendricks, who is looking for a job in technology – an industry that accounts for 31% of all job cuts announced this year, according to a Challenger, Gray & Christmas report.
The nation's unemployment rate was relatively low at 4.1% in July, though economists say it fell this year largely because workers dropped out of the labor force, not because a massive number of candidates were hired. Some analysts believe one reason behind the departures is burnout from the job search.
After being laid off in May 2023, Candee Petersen said she secured a new job three months later in August, only to be laid off again in September. Though she has landed some contract work since, Petersen, 54, said that about 5,000 applications later, she’s still without a full-time job. A former executive administrative business partner, she first applied to similar roles. As time dragged on, she applied to “everything” that aligned with her skill set. 
Amanda Dobson, founder of Clear Pathways, an HR and leadership advisory firm, said one of the problems with hiring today may be that “volume has replaced intention” for job seekers and recruiters alike. 
The explosion of remote work amid the COVID-19 pandemic led to more applicants vying for the same jobs around the country rather than competing with just those in their area or those willing to relocate for a position, Dobson said. 
U.S. applicants per open role have doubled since spring 2022, according to LinkedIn research released at the start of this year. It found that 65% of people said finding a job had become more challenging, citing competition as the main obstacle.
Recruiters used to review about 100 applications per role in early 2021. They are now processing 291 per hire, according to a 2026 Talent Trends report by Ashby, a recruiting platform. 
Employers receive hundreds of applications and cannot respond to each. Applicants don’t hear back, so they apply for more jobs, and the cycle continues, Dobson said. 
Vicki Salemi, a career expert at job listings site Monster, said she has seen lifeguards apply to certified public accountant roles and a candidate apply to 10 positions at the same company at one time, a “red flag” to the employer. She and Dobson advise job seekers to narrow their search. 
If you’re “sending out 500 applications as a candidate, there’s no way that you actually want to work for those 500 jobs,” Dobson said. “Instead of spending the energy – the five to 10 minutes per application that you’re sending out – and sending it out 500 times, take 20 minutes on your resume and take 20 minutes on your application to thoughtfully answer the application questions.” 
A problem with that approach may be that only 10% of job seekers feel that most of their applications are reviewed by a human, according to LiveCareer’s 2025 Job Search Frustration Survey, though 64% believed their resumes were optimized for applicant tracking systems.
Dobson said they’re right to be skeptical. 
“I talk with a lot of HR professionals, and in the recruitment process, they’re not looking at every single resume,” she said. “They’re letting the systems do that.” 
At 93%, most recruiters surveyed by LinkedIn late last year said they planned to increase their AI use in 2026, and 59% said the technology was already helping them discover new candidates. Some have gone so far as to introduce AI interviewers. 
“Nobody talks to you anymore. You get invitations, ‘Oh, we have an AI interview.' I’m not talking to AI. I’m not sending a Loom video just so you can see me sitting there talking to myself,” Petersen said. “Where’s the humanity in this?” 
But applicants are using AI, too. The LinkedIn survey found 81% of people had used AI to assist their job search or said they planned to. Hendricks described it as an “automation arms race.” 
“Applicants are increasingly using AI to tailor their resumes and cover letters on the applications, and employers increasingly will use AI or other automated systems to filter and rank those applications,” he said. 
Hendricks is not relying on AI to rewrite his applications, however. 
“Even before AI, there was this concept that you should have a unique resume for every job application,” he said. “Now, AI has made that easier, whereas people used to do it by hand. But I mean, my experience is my experience. I’m not going to augment it in ways that aren’t true.” 
Even if an applicant uses AI to help optimize their application, if a tracking system is flagging resumes for specific keywords or phrases, a misplaced comma may be the reason an applicant is never seriously considered, Dobson said. 
“Because of where a comma was placed in their criteria, they were auto-rejecting a lot of candidates that they should have been reviewing,” she said of one company. “We’re relying too heavily on these systems and not using a human to review candidates.” 
The usual way people measure job market tightness is by comparing the number of open jobs with the number of unemployed people. 
However, “a large and rising share of job postings target already-employed workers or represent inactive listings,” a Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis analysis said last November. “As a result, the vacancy-to-unemployment ratio now overstates how tight the labor market truly is for unemployed workers.” 
Even if a job listing is not a scam, a company may post a role while planning to hire internally or recruiting someone already employed elsewhere. A budget change could mean a role that was once real is now cut. In those cases, a company may advertise an opening that job seekers never had a realistic chance of getting. Candidates call these listings “ghost jobs.” 
Another Ashby report released last year analyzing data from its customer base found that 82% of open jobs in 2024 led to a real hire but that 5.5% were paused, 1% led to an offer but no hire, and 3.5% were pulled without a reason given. 
Applicants “have no way to know whether another candidate was selected, or the hiring plan changed, or the position was never actively being filled, and it was just a listing to increase the appearance of success on the part of the company,” Hendricks said. “If I apply to 10 positions and receive no interviews, I need to know whether that means I’m targeting the wrong roles, or I’m presenting my experience poorly, or I’m applying to positions that weren't realistic opportunities in the first place.” 
Salemi said that while it’s true there are job postings unemployed candidates never had a realistic chance of getting, companies ""don’t post them just for fun” but rather to build a talent pipeline or stack of resumes to reference the next time they have an opening. 
Talent pipelines haven’t worked out for Petersen. 
“Nobody ever has called me back because they built a pipeline from a job that I applied to three years ago,” Petersen said.
Candidates have more trust in job postings when companies share clear salary ranges as well as transparent expectations around workload and hours, a new Monster report found. But they don’t always get them. 
After reviewing enough job posts that listed the position as remote only to find out the company requires employees to work in office, Petersen doesn’t trust job descriptions anymore. 
She’s not alone. Only 10% of workers say job descriptions “always” accurately reflect the reality of the role, according to the Monster report. It found that 81% of workers surveyed said they at least sometimes encounter job descriptions that “feel vague or overly polished.” 
Beyond determining whether a company means what it says in a job posting, candidates also are navigating scam offers. Petersen said she receives suspicious texts and emails promising to help her land a job “every day.” 
“You get, ‘Of course. Yeah. We have a job for you, but you need to pay this person to rewrite your resume,’” she said. “It’s scary to see how many people will try to take advantage of somebody who’s already down.” 
At 95%, most job seekers have encountered a suspicious job offer, and 98% said today's job market increases vulnerability to scams, a separate Monster report found.
To verify whether an opportunity is real, Salemi said, job seekers should ask themselves a few questions: Is it realistic? Does it highlight specific responsibilities? Is it listed on the company website? Does the company have a mailing address? Does the recruiter have a professional email address? 
She reminds job seekers that most companies won’t need their Social Security number or address until after they are onboarded. 
“Legitimate employers will not just immediately hire you without having screened you,” Salemi said. “Your gut was likely going to be right anyway. So trust it.”
Reach Rachel Barber at rbarber@usatoday.com, follow her on X @rachelbarber_, and subscribe to her newsletter ""Making More of Your Money"" here"
60,2026-08-21,"Autonomous robot

Robots and autonomous machines on peaqOS can now verify whether they are interacting with a real human through World ID, using zero-knowledge proofs instead of names, faces, or documents. The integration, available through robotic.sh, requires no API key or verification fee. A medication delivery scenario demonstrates the concept: a patient verifies at order, a pharmacist verifies at loading, and the recipient proves they are the same human at delivery, all without the robot learning anyone’s identity.
It lets autonomous machines confirm they are interacting with real people without learning their identities
August 21, 2026: Robots and autonomous machines running peaqOS can now verify whether they are interacting with a real human through World ID, following a new integration available through robotic.sh.
The integration brings proof of human directly into peaqOS, allowing machines to request and verify World ID proofs without requiring an API key or charging a fee for each verification.
The development addresses a growing challenge as robots move beyond controlled industrial environments and begin interacting directly with people. Delivery robots, shared machines and other autonomous systems increasingly need ways to determine whether the person requesting or completing an action is a real human, while avoiding unnecessary collection of personal information.
World ID approaches the problem using zero-knowledge proofs. Instead of providing a robot with a person’s name, face or identity document, a user can generate a proof through World App demonstrating that they are a real, unique human.
In certain interactions, the system can also establish that the person completing an action is the same human who initiated it, without revealing that person’s underlying identity.
That capability could become particularly relevant for autonomous delivery.
In a medication delivery scenario demonstrating the integration, a patient verifies through World App before an order is accepted. The robot receives a zero-knowledge proof rather than identifying information about the patient.
At the pharmacy, the pharmacist verifies before placing the medication inside the robot and sealing its compartment. When the robot arrives at its destination, the patient verifies again. The system can then establish that the recipient is the same human associated with the original order before allowing the compartment to unlock.
The model offers an alternative to PINs, pickup codes, user accounts and conventional identity verification checks. Codes can potentially be transferred or stolen, while traditional identity verification can require companies to collect and retain personal information. World ID instead allows the machine to receive a cryptographic proof.
peaqOS provides the coordination infrastructure that allows machines to access and use World ID. Robots can use peaq decentralized identifiers for machine identity, discover the service through the Machine Market and coordinate the verification process from request through result.
The interaction can also generate a verifiable and auditable record showing that verification occurred while keeping proof metadata redacted. This gives machine operators a way to establish that a verification took place without requiring the robot to collect the person’s identity.
The integration could also support situations where machines need to establish human uniqueness rather than identity. A robot distributing promotional products, for example, could use World ID to enforce a one-item-per-human rule without maintaining a database containing the identities of recipients.
Shared robots and machines represent another potential application. Instead of requiring someone to create an account and provide personal information before accessing a machine, operators could use World ID to establish that a user is a real, unique person while minimizing the amount of personal data collected.
World ID is now available through robotic.sh for robots and machines running peaqOS, extending privacy-preserving proof of human into interactions between people and autonomous systems.

63,2026-08-21,"Logomania may be ""out"" in fashion, but that's certainly not the case in beauty.
Wearing a monogram on your face thanks to under-eye masks and patches has increasingly become a flex. These little patches, once reserved for the hidden stages of getting ready in the bathroom, are now vehicles for self-expression, as professionals wear them while running errands, grabbing a cup of coffee, or even posting selfies online.
""Under-eye masks have been a staple in beauty for years, but they've evolved from purely functional skincare products into cultural and social beauty moments,"" Nicolette Bosco, vice president and divisional merchandise manager for Macy's Beauty, told Business Insider.
Over the past few years, under-eye patches have evolved into branded, graphic patches that have become beauty status symbols. Chanel and Dior are early examples, releasing their patches in 2018 and 2022, respectively. These two luxury fashion brands, with some of the most recognizable logos in the world, unleashed their monograms in skincare and never looked back.
Today, under-eye patches are like the beauty version of carrying a hot handbag, whether they're cult favorites or widely beloved. A Topicals mask might be like a Luar Ana bag on your face; a Dior patch might channel the skincare-version vibes of a Saddle.
And like a bag, they're not just for wearing IRL — whether that's a chill house party, a swanky first class seat, or a workout class — but also for posting online. Their popularity is the latest evolution in social skincare, or the current era where once-private routines are not only public but also worth flaunting.
There's an affordability factor as well. Designer-brand eye patches are a more accessible way to wear the coveted branding. 
Dior's five-pack sells for $84, which costs much less than the several-thousand-dollar Saddle Bag. A 10-pack of Chanel's patches goes for $120, which is still a far cry from the brand's infamous, more-than-$10,000 Flap Bags.
Still, it's not only about the flashy designer logos. Today's most beloved graphic under-eye patches also include Dieux Skin, which uniquely has reusable ones, Hailey Bieber's Rhode Beauty, Selena Gomez's Rare Beauty, and Topicals. They all sell patches with the logo treatment, and they're all bestsellers at Sephora.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
Topicals, for example, which debuted its $22 under-eye masks in November 2022, remain among the brightest on the market thanks to its bright pink hue. Topicals founder and CEO Olamide Olowe said she wanted customers to feel like they were wearing something cultural, not just another skincare product.
""We were really inspired by streetwear and the way that clothing and accessories can become signals of identity and community. We wanted to bring that same energy into skincare,"" Olowe told Business Insider.
None of these patches is simply for show; each claims an antiaging benefit. Rhode's peptides plump away the appearance of fine lines; Dior's hyaluronic acid hydrates the skin; and Topicals' niacinamide lessens the look of hyperpigmentation.
Creating something functional is only part of the equation for brands. Developing an eye patch that customers want to wear outside is now a major part of the challenge.
""There was a time when these rituals were very private,"" Summer Nacewicz, Alo's executive vice president of marketing and creative, said. ""Wearing an eye mask out is a little signal that says, 'I'm taking care of myself, and I'm not hiding it.'""
""Now people wear their eye masks to coffee, planes, on their morning walk… and of course, they post them,"" she added.
This is what inspired Alo to launch its own Bio NAD+ Recovery Eye Masks, each dotted with Alo's classic sans-serif and lowercase lettering, this summer. 
""At our Alo Wellness Clubs, we started noticing people coming in first thing in the morning already wearing eye masks,"" Nacewicz said. ""It signaled something bigger: Beauty and recovery rituals that used to happen behind closed doors were becoming something people were comfortable taking out into the world.""
These patches have quickly become popular among influencers and beauty aficionados as a way to luxurify and romanticize skincare routines. It wasn't long before patches began flooding algorithms. Graphic eye masks ""visual appeal makes them highly shareable on social media,"" Bosco added. (Think Starface, with its bright, star-shaped pimple patches, famously adored by Gen Z.)
To Olowe, this is fundamentally about a shift in values. ""There's something powerful about being seen trying… whether that's trying a new product, taking care of your skin, or simply expressing yourself,"" she said.
No comments right now, check back later.

64,2026-08-21,"The 9-to-5 lifestyle never quite suited Kelan Kline. His first job out of college in 2012 was in insurance sales and involved cold calling.
""It lasted about two weeks,"" he told Business Insider. Quick corporate stints became ""kind of a recurring story for me.""
He cycled through several jobs before becoming a jail deputy in Rochester, New York. By 2016, he and his wife, Brittany, were newly married, had bought a starter home, and were paying down roughly $40,000 in student-loan debt.
""There was a lot of financial burden on us,"" he said. Plus, they wanted more control over their time.
The couple started looking into businesses that could eventually replace their salaries. They landed on a personal finance blog partly because the startup costs were minimal: they launched The Savvy Couple with a web-hosting plan that cost $2.95 a month.
The potential upside also appealed to Kelan.
""With an online business, it's unlimited scalability because you're reaching the entire world,"" he said. At first, however, they weren't reaching anybody. ""It was tough. We were starting from scratch. We didn't have any tech background.""
For nine months, the blog generated no revenue. Then they made $50 from a sponsored post.
It was enough to convince Kelan that their site had potential, and about two weeks after that first paycheck landed, he left his job to focus fully on the business. The Savvy Couple eventually grew enough for Brittany to leave her teaching job in 2019 and join it full time.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
In 2023, their business surpassed $1 million in annual revenue for the first time. Profit-and-loss statements from Savvy Media Marketing, reviewed by Business Insider, reported total sales of about $1.3 million in 2023. In 2024, they brought in about $1.1 million.
Over the years, the Klines said they've used four primary methods to monetize their online businesses.
1. Display advertising. According to the Klines, one of the simplest ways to monetize a website with significant traffic is through display advertising. Think: visual online ads, such as banners, images, or videos.
The way it works is, a publisher — website owners or app makers — can join an advertising network that places ads throughout the site. The publisher is then typically compensated based on how many people view those ads.
For the Klines, the appeal was that once the traffic existed, display advertising as an income source was ""really passive."" The limitation is that the model depends heavily on maintaining an audience.
2. Affiliate marketing. Affiliate marketing allows a creator to earn a commission for recommending another company's product or service.
The Klines, for example, have written articles that review or compare products and services. When a reader follows an affiliate link, ""you get paid a commission based on either them signing up or purchasing,"" they explained.
It can work particularly well for creators whose audiences already turn to them for recommendations. However, like display advertising, affiliate marketing requires a reliable way to reach potential customers. If website traffic disappears, which the Klines have dealt with over the past couple of years, affiliate revenue can decline with it.
3. Sponsorships. Once a creator builds an audience, ""there'll be companies that want to get in front of your audience because you have their ideal audience's attention and trust,"" Kelan said.
A sponsorship might be a one-time campaign or an ongoing arrangement in which a company pays a recurring fee.
4. Selling your own products and services. The Klines have increasingly favored a fourth strategy: selling something they control themselves. That can include courses, memberships, digital downloads, or coaching.
Brittany has taken the lead on The Savvy Mama, an offshoot of their original site focused on household finances, meal planning, and family routines. She said the key to creating products has been identifying specific problems her audience already wants solved.
""We didn't make products just for the fun of it,"" she said. ""The audience needed something. They had a problem, and then the products that I created solved the problem.""
One common issue among her readers was deciding what to feed their families, so she created a membership that provides meal plans and recipes. The membership costs $9 a month and had about 1,000 members at the time of the interview, according to the Klines.
Digital products can require substantial work up front, but once they're built, there's not a ton of maintenance required, she added: ""You create it, and then market it, and let it do the work.""
The Klines still believe online businesses can offer low startup costs, flexibility, and significant room to scale, but if they were starting from scratch in 2026, they wouldn't build a traditional blog that depends primarily on Google search traffic.
""The big lesson we learned from both of our blogs losing traffic is the importance of owning the business and not being reliant on one traffic form,"" Kelan said.
They're now focusing more on digital products, memberships, YouTube, and a marketing agency that helps local businesses. Kelan particularly likes models under which customers pay month after month.
The broader principle, he said, is to do more of the work up front — whether that means building a useful digital product or setting up systems for a business client — and then create a revenue stream that can continue over time.
No comments right now, check back later.
Comments are unavailable right now.

65,2026-08-21,"On June 30, Paul Atkins, chairman of the Securities and Exchange Commission, stood before the Economic Club of New York and delivered a history lesson. With the nation’s 250th birthday days away, Mr. Atkins told his audience that the Declaration of Independence and Adam Smith’s “Wealth of Nations,” both products of 1776, rest on “the same conviction: trust the individual, not the institution.” America’s founding documents, he said, “in many respects, reflect Smith’s central themes,” and the founders, wary of concentrated power “whether lodged in a crown, in a parliament, or in a bureaucracy,” built around liberty a governing framework “as light as prudence would permit.”
The problem is that Mr. Atkins turns the coincidence of 1776 into kinship, and kinship into influence. He then conscripts that invented founding into a deregulatory agenda, capped by a wholesale retreat from cryptocurrency enforcement, that America’s founders would have recognized as a corruption of republican government.
Start with the Declaration. Mr. Atkins’s most concrete evidence is Jefferson’s well-worn copy of “The Wealth of Nations.” But the Monticello source cited in his own footnote reports that Jefferson acquired the book while serving in France between 1784 and 1789, at least eight years after he drafted the Declaration. Jefferson may have encountered Smith’s ideas before 1776, but there is no evidence that “The Wealth of Nations” shaped the Declaration. On the contrary, Jefferson told James Madison in 1823 that he “turned to neither book nor pamphlet” while writing it. Asked by Henry Lee in 1825 about its sources, he described the Declaration as “an expression of the American mind,” reflecting the “harmonising sentiments of the day” embodied in “the elementary books of public right, as Aristotle, Cicero, Locke, Sidney, etc.”
By fusing America’s founding to “The Wealth of Nations,” Mr. Atkins turns Smith’s defense of free markets into a justification for weakening public oversight of politically favored financial interests. Notice that Atkins’ catalog of dangers includes crowns, parliaments and bureaucracies, but omits the economic factions the founders also recognized as potential threats to republican government. James Madison warned in Federalist No. 10 that “the most common and durable source of factions has been the various and unequal distribution of property,” specifically identifying the landed, manufacturing, mercantile and moneyed interests. Jefferson made the point even more explicitly. Writing in 1816, he called for the nation to “crush in its birth the aristocracy of our monied corporations, which dare already to challenge our government to a trial of strength and bid defiance to the laws of our country.”
The founders valued private property and encouraged commerce. But they also recognized a basic republican principle that Mr. Atkins ignores: power can threaten liberty whether it is wielded by the state or by private interests wealthy enough to bend the state to their will.
That missing half of the founding tradition becomes impossible to ignore when Mr. Atkins turns to cryptocurrency. He boasted that the S.E.C. is answering President Trump’s call “to make America the Crypto Capital of the World.” In practice, that has meant dismissing, or settling on favorable terms for the defendant, the majority of outstanding cryptocurrency enforcement actions, several of them involving defendants with business ties to the president or his family.
The crypto industry helped underwrite the political conditions for this solicitude. It was the top corporate donor in the 2024 election cycle and has already amassed a nine-figure campaign arsenal for the coming midterms. And the president is personally invested in the outcome. His own financial disclosure reports that he earned more than $1.4 billion in income from his family’s crypto ventures in 2025.
Mr. Atkins’s crypto agenda is difficult to reconcile with his tribute to Adam Smith, because Smith did not regard money and banking as a realm beyond public law. He welcomed privately issued bank notes redeemable in gold or silver, recognizing them as an efficient means of facilitating commerce. But when Scottish banks issued small-denomination notes whose failure would fall hardest on poor laborers, Smith endorsed restricting them, conceding that the rule violated “natural liberty” but defending it as “exactly of the same kind” as requiring party walls to stop the spread of fire.
The Constitution reflects a similar instinct. Having witnessed the paper currency issued by the Continental Congress depreciate into worthlessness, the Framers vested authority over the nation’s monetary system in Congress, giving it the power to “coin Money” and “regulate the Value thereof,” while forbidding the states to coin money, issue bills of credit or make anything but gold and silver legal tender. They did not prohibit private bank notes, but they made clear that establishing the nation’s monetary framework was a public responsibility. In different ways, Smith and the Framers reached the same conclusion: private monetary innovation has a place, but it must remain subject to public law. Mr. Atkins’s crypto agenda is difficult to reconcile with that principle.
Mr. Atkins closed with a warning about socialism, in an unmistakable shot at Mayor Zohran Mamdani and other New York leaders who, he said, “are beginning to speak the language of control rather than of freedom.” He even quoted President Trump’s warning that under communism “great violence proceeds at levels never seen before.” But those warnings ring hollow coming from an administration that has repeatedly intervened in private markets, including by taking ownership stakes in private companies, while extending preferential treatment to politically connected firms. A warning about political violence likewise loses its force when its cited authority incited a mob to halt the peaceful transfer of power and then pardoned participants who beat police officers.
If Mr. Atkins is looking for a lesson about socialism, he might begin with the history of the agency he leads. American communism attracted its largest organized following during the Great Depression, after a stock market rife with fraud and manipulation, and lacking comprehensive federal oversight, crashed and helped drag the economy down with it. Congress responded with the Securities Act of 1933 and the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, creating the S.E.C. Critics denounced the legislation as socialism. Congressman Fred Britten complained that “the real object” of the 1934 law “is to Russianize everything.” But the republic survived and American capital markets went on to finance the American century under the very rules Mr. Atkins now portrays as shackles.
Mr. Atkins wants the founders to bless a world in which public power retreats and private financial power is presumed innocent. The actual founding will not cooperate. Jefferson feared a monied aristocracy, Madison feared faction, and Smith accepted restraints on banking when private liberty endangered society.
The S.E.C. was created to restore confidence in financial markets after their collapse had helped make socialism politically attractive. Well-regulated markets produce trust. Trust attracts investment, and broadly shared prosperity remains among the strongest defenses against political extremism. Markets retain their legitimacy only when the public believes the rules apply equally to everyone. An S.E.C. chairman who retreats from enforcing the securities laws against a politically connected industry tells ordinary Americans that wealth and influence purchase different rules. Once that belief takes hold, confidence in markets erodes, and critics of capitalism scarcely need to make their own case.
 
Lee Reiners is a lecturing fellow at Duke University

66,2026-08-21,"Today's best desktop PCs can be put to use in a number of ways, from daily office work, to gaming, and from media consumption to its creation. This is also true of all of the best laptops on the market. These machines are packed with so much power, connectivity and portability that it can be easy to overlook the fact that this wasn't always the case. A lot of PCs have come and gone over the years, paving the way for what we have now.
The 1970s saw a lot of defining moments. A president resigned amid a scandal, the Vietnam War came to an end, and many different kinds of technology moved out of research labs and into our homes. There are lots of electronics from the 1960s and 1970s that shaped an entire generation, but one that really gained footing in the 1970s was the personal computer. Enough advancement took place for computers to shrink to desktop sizes, and with the help of the emerging video game market, more and more consumers were becoming interested.
While it took a good chunk of the decade for the personal computer to fully grab the American people's attention, several models helped define the decade both technologically and culturally. Some of them only existed within research and development departments, while many others became household names. Each of them made a mark on the decade, whether through advancing the marketplace or through the relationship their users came to develop with them.
While the Altair 8800 had no keyboard, no monitor, and no modern day interface, it played a major role in the public beginning to embrace the personal computer. The idea of a computer in every home took time for the marketplace to embrace, and the Altair 8800's design is somewhat reminiscent of why. It utilized blinking lights to communicate with the user, who could tell it what to do through switches on the machine's front panel.
It wasn't a particularly user-friendly machine, nor was the Altair 8800 popular in terms of sales. But it's often recognized as the computer that served as the starting point of the personal computer age. In 1975, the 8800 appeared on the cover of Popular Electronics magazine, and for the first time the computer was presented to hobbyists and electronics enthusiasts no longer as a machine reserved for giant corporations and research institutions.
The Altair 8800 served mostly as a stepping stone to computers that would hit the market eventually. The switch-and-light interface would soon give way to more user-friendly designs. But at the time of its release, commercial computers were generally priced in the $30,000 range. The Altair 8800 cost just $439, announcing to the hobbyist market that owning a personal computer was within their reach.
Another personal computer that can make a claim for being the machine that kicked off the PC revolution of the late 1970s is the Xerox Alto. It was developed in 1973, two years before the Altair 8800 hit the market, and introduced some features that would become commonplace among PCs. The Xerox Alto utilized a graphical user interface, which we continue to use today in the form of desktop icons, docks, and software application windows.
This interface was a massive overhaul in the computing world, and it would inspire software designers for decades. In the 1970s, it influenced many companies, including some that would go on to take over the entire market in both Apple and Microsoft. Developed at the Xerox Palo Alto Research Center, the Alto completely changed the relationship between the computer and users. It combined a mouse with the new visual interface, allowing users to simply click in order to interact.
Unable to manufacture the machine at a consumer price, Xerox never took the Alto to market. It cost around $12,000 to build, and could have cost as much as $40,000 as a retail product. But the research and development behind the Alto is why it's often recognized as the first PC. It directly led to decisions Steve Jobs would make in regard to computers made by Apple, which wasn't far behind Xerox in making its mark on the 1970s.
While today Apple makes a MacBook for every budget, in the 1970s its computer lineup wasn't quite so robust. In 1977, it was still mostly hobbyists and electronics enthusiasts who were drawn to personal computing, but in the same way Apple revolutionized mobile computing with the iPhone, it did so for personal computing with the Apple II. As would become a staple of the Apple brand, the computer featured a sleek look and was designed to be user friendly.
What stood out visually about the Apple II was its use of color. It had built-in color graphics, which was still rare at the time. Apple paired the Apple II with a color monitor. This helped catch the eye of everyday people who were becoming more and more aware that computers were making their way into individual homes. The Apple II also sold with a keyboard and built-in audio, making it a convenient and approachable all-in-one purchasing option.
Software was another major influence the Apple II had on the PC revolution of the 1970s. In 1979, a company called Software Arts introduced VisiCalc, the first spreadsheet application ever made, and released it specifically for the Apple II. This made the machine enticing as a professional tool as well. Apple updated the Apple II over the next few years, and by 1984, had sold more than two million units.
The Apple II was among three computers that make up what has become known as ""The 1977 Trinity."" The TRS-80 was the third machine among them, but the first in the Trinity was the Commodore PET 2001. The name was an acronym for Personal Electronic Transactor. It had an all-in-one construction that included a monitor, keyboard and internal storage, and it utilized a 1 Mhz MOS 6502 processor. It was the first computer Commodore ever made.
Nowadays, Commodore is one of many extinct PC brands, but the PET 2001 made it one of the most recognized computer brands of the 1970s. Similarly to the Apple II, it made itself appealing to prospective buyers through the convenience of an all-in-one purchase. The PET 2001 had a built-in cassette that could be used for data storage. Software was another selling point, with the inclusion of BASIC allowing users to write their own programs.
Commodore sold the PET 2001 for $495 upon its release in 1977, and while it was extremely popular at the time, it's most recognized for helping to open the doors to the homes of individual computer users. While it sold well, the PET 2001 drew criticism for its small keyboard and monitor, and with both built directly into the machine's design, users had to take what they got without swappable alternatives.
Due in large part to the commercial success of computers like the Apple II and Commodore PET 2001, other major electronics brands became interested in entering the PC market. One such company was Atari, which had spent most of the 1970s establishing its name not only making video game consoles, but pioneering the video game market. In late 1978 the company released the Atari 800, as well as its smaller sibling, the Atari 400.
Both were built around the MOS 6502 — the same processor the Commodore PET 2001 used — but Atari set out to position itself as a gaming option within the PC market. Personal computes were yet to provide hardware that could handle more than daily office computation, but Atari built custom chips that removed certain functions from the CPU load, leaving more power to play video games on the machine.
While Atari continued its work in the video console space, the 400 and 800 sparked a line of Atari 8-bit computers that are now somewhat renowned among retro gaming and computer enthusiasts. These included the Atari 1200XL, which was a commercial flop upon its release in the spring of 1983, and the Atari 1000, which focused on high-end components and never made it to market. The Atari 400 and Atari 800 helped carry the PC explosion into the 1980s, though the company struggled to keep up with the PC market's then-emerging giants."
67,2026-08-21,Here’s what could be next for Marvell stock.
68,2026-08-21,"ByBill Holmes,
Forbes Books Author.
Bill Holmes warns against blaming market conditions for missed sales targets, stressing the importance of realistic forecasting. He highlights the ""pump and dump"" danger of over-optimistic projections driven by external pressure, which often leads to business failure. Accurate forecasts, he argues, must consider numerous factors like product strength, market conditions, and team performance. Even significant market shifts, such as recessions, can be navigated by adjusting margins, as ""margin elasticity"" allows businesses to recover without losing market share. Ultimately, underperformance often signals leadership deficiencies, including poor recruitment and inadequate team management, rather than external market forces. Leaders must ensure achievable budgets and hold teams accountable.
As soon as I hear one of my leaders in a sales review trying to blame the market for not meeting their budget numbers, the alarm bells begin ringing. 
This is because at the point we agree on our annual forecasts, I never try to pressure management to put forward volumes and margins that are unachievable, and I always try to put myself in their shoes and consider what I think I’d be capable of delivering. Obviously, the biggest guide to these numbers is the last 12 months’ performance, especially the current run rate, overlaid with the additional sales and marketing investment we plan to make going forward.
Many leaders in other businesses I have come across over the years have obviously not had this forecasting discipline, and this getting-ahead-of-themselves phenomenon is sometimes because maybe they want to impress the external market, or they are being pressured and encouraged by advisers or bankers who actually have no idea what is realistically achievable. That usually ends badly and often fatally for the business, and there is a long list of companies that have gone this way over the last few years after some crazy early valuations. The often-used slang for this phenomenon is “pump and dump!”
For me, forecasts must be made very carefully and take many different factors into account if I am going to sign them off and put my reputation on the line, as they ultimately feed into our overall plan. These factors include: 
If this is all done correctly, even significant market changes, such as a country moving from growth to recession and a swing of a few percentage points of GDP, should have little effect on our business. If there are small changes that need adjustment, the levers our management team has at its disposal can be pulled to make sure we stay on track. 
A good example of this type of midyear change is our response to the several recessions I’ve experienced over the years, during which our bad debt numbers always increase as business insolvencies rise, especially in the SME sector. This might mean a sudden additional cost to the business of 0.10, 0.20, or even up to 0.50 ppl across a whole country’s volume, which could add up to several million pounds over a year if it’s one of our larger countries. However, I do not accept this as an explanation for the underperformance of our net profit numbers, and I expect my team members to increase their margins to offset this shortfall. Experience has shown me that if everyone in the market feels the same pain, then margin elasticity occurs, and we can recover our numbers without losing market position. If we didn’t operate this way, our financial performance would decline, putting us under pressure with bankers and suppliers, as well as some unhappy shareholders.
Hence, for me, the answer to underperformance in a particular part of our operations over the years has normally been that the team there isn’t good enough, and we need to make some changes. The drops tend to come hand in hand with being under-recruited, and the specific leader has failed to understand that if they don’t operate on or close to the head count, then they have no chance of meeting their budget targets, however good their individual salespeople are. 
Good leaders always have stronger management teams under them and consequently have reduced staff churn and better overall performance. They also spend a larger percentage of their working week on recruiting and people development, as well as on managing poor performers out of the business. So, don’t agree to budgets you don’t think are achievable, and don’t let your team blame the market for its poor performance."

							Suze Orman's June 2026 advice to dollar-cost average into SPCX near $200 left followers underwater as the stock dropped to $136 and drew a fresh Sell initiation.													

							SpaceX posted a $541 million quarterly loss against $18.37 billion in capex, while a lockup expiration released 911 million new shares into the market.

Are you ahead, or behind on retirement? SmartAsset's free tool can match you with a financial advisor in minutes to help you answer that today. Each advisor has been carefully vetted, and must act in your best interests. Don't waste another minute; learn more here.

					Sending You to Google News in 3

© Leigh Vogel / Stringer / Getty Images North America
On her June 18, 2026 podcast, Suze Orman told listeners that if they wanted exposure to Elon Musk’s newly public rocket company, the cleanest way was to dollar cost average into the stock itself, symbol SPCX, at around $200. That day, SpaceX (NASDAQ:SPCX | SPCX Price Prediction) closed at $185. This morning it changed hands near $136, and an analyst desk flagged a fresh Sell initiation on the name, one of several bearish calls now on the Street.
If you followed Orman’s advice and put $500 a month into SPCX starting June 18, you are underwater on nearly every tranche. Orman’s core audience is retirees and near-retirees who trust her voice on capital preservation. A single-stock DCA into a pre-profit, capex-heavy IPO is a different animal than what most of that audience signed up for.
Dollar cost averaging reduces timing risk in a diversified portfolio. It is a poor tool for reducing valuation risk in a single stock. Orman conflated these different problems.
If you committed $500 a month for the three months since that podcast, and SPCX went from $185 on June 18 to about $136 today, your average cost sits somewhere in the mid-$150s. That is a 28% drawdown on your first buy and a smaller loss on later ones. DCA cushioned the pain but did not eliminate it. The deeper question is whether the business supports the price at any of those levels.
The Q2 report provides the raw material. SpaceX beat on revenue at $7.81 billion versus $6.93 billion expected, with Starlink and AI leading the mix. The $541 million net loss and the $18.37 billion of capital spending in a single quarter, $15.83 billion of it on AI compute, tell the other side of the story. A company burning that much cash to build data centers is a venture bet with a ticker, categorically different from a bond substitute (we profiled seven suppliers riding that same buildout, from power to cooling, in a free AI infrastructure report).
Reddit noticed what Orman did not price in. On August 7, roughly 911.5 million shares held by employees and early investors became eligible to trade. As one poster put it, “That’s more than the roughly 639M shares sold in the IPO”. Buying near the highs before that supply event is the opposite of what dollar cost averaging protects against.
The single factor determining whether Orman’s advice was reckless or merely aggressive is what percentage of your portfolio you put into it.
Consider two listeners. Listener A hears “dollar cost average” and puts 2% of a $500,000 portfolio into SPCX at $185. The stock falls to $136. The loss is roughly $2,650, or half a percent of the total account. Annoying, not life-changing. Listener B puts 25% of the same portfolio in. Same drawdown, but now the account is down more than $33,000, and the retirement math bends.
Both followed the exact same advice. One is fine. One is not. Orman’s on-air instruction did not specify a cap, and that omission is where the harm lives.
At least one member of Congress, Rep. Timmons, disclosed a SpaceX purchase on July 18, 2026, and this morning Musk himself was busy retweeting a post calling X “the nervous system of the world” rather than addressing the stock. Both are noise around the ticker, a reminder that the loudest voices on a stock rarely have your retirement in mind.
Dollar cost averaging manages timing risk, not the risk that you picked the wrong stock at the wrong price.

Grand Theft Auto 6GTA 6 
It’s been three days since a leaker calling themself “Cyberleek” began drip-feeding footage of Grand Theft Auto 6 to the public, seemingly in retribution for Rockstar Games’ digital release strategy. But despite the extremity of the leaks around what stands to be the biggest game of the decade, the leaker does not appear to have been either caught or stopped yet; they even leaked even more footage just this morning!Â Even so, those following the leaks seem to think that it’s only a matter of time before the culprit is caught by Rockstar and Take-Two, in part based on how easy it seems to be to track down the leaker’s primary crypto wallet.
This comes from a lengthy post on the GTAForums by user Vice Cit, who did an extremely detailed deep dive tracing Cyberleek’s crypto wallet and breaking down what information they could glean from it. Their analysis comes in two parts and is pretty interesting, but I’ll sum it up here if you don’t want to get into the weeds on the inner workings of some guy’s crypto scheme. As Vice Cit points out, while the leaker claims their activities are an effort to pressure Rockstar into moving away from digital pre-orders, since day one they have been using the leaks to ask for money in the form of crypto currency. “The gamers rights messaging was just virtue signaling to gain public support from people who were upset about the loss of physical media,” Vice Cit writes. “The person behind this leak is making a substantial amount of money and I think after reading this post you’ll see that we may be in for a longer ride than we initially realize. This isn’t the 2022 leak, this has been planned out and executed very intentionally to maximize the money they’ll make from this.”
Cyberleek has created their own crypto coin, creatively referred to as $CYBERLEEK, which they are marketing to viewers alongside each new leak. According to Vice Cit, Cyberleek invested about $29,000 into creating the coin. Though they don’t make money directly from its sale, every time someone buys $CYBERLEEK, there is an additional transaction fee that goes directly to the creator, which is how the leaker is actually profiting here. Vice Cit suggests they probably made about $30,000 on fees on the first day of the leak, essentially making back what they put in, and since then have been getting several thousand per day.
The creator also apparently has 270 million tokens set aside in an additional, private wallet that was worth about $391k at the time of Vice Cit’s post, which could conceivably be sold off for profit as well. Vice Cit says the smart thing to do here would be to sell these off slowly so it doesn’t cause issues for the coin market, but it’s possible there’s a larger strategy at play here for what these coins are for.
With this strategy, Vice Cit suggests, the leaker is likely incentivized to keep the leaks coming steadily each day, to keep the coin relevant and keep people interested in trading it. However, they may not be able to do this much longer. Vice Cit claims they’ve been able to trace the wallet that created the coin back to a KuCoin wallet, and KuCoin’s policy allows law enforcement with a subpoena to receive identity information about account holders. Given that Take-Two is already subpoenaing Discord and Microsoft for records regarding this leak, if Vice Cit has done their homework and the leaker is this easy to trace, it seems likely that subpoenas of the crypto companies won’t be far behind.
And it’s likely, too, that Take-Two is in a big hurry to get this wrapped up soon, so the leaks won’t further undermine the big official Netflix reveal of Grand Theft Auto 6 next week. Whatever happens, I guess this person better hope that whatever money they made off the crypto here was worth the lawyer fees.

Nokia is the brand behind the best-selling mobile phone of all time, but now it's disappearing from the smartphone arena altogether. The Nokia G310 5G is losing software support on August 24, 2026, and the Nokia XR21 is losing software support on May 24, 2027. These are the last two Nokia smartphones that are actively being supported, and after those respective dates, the lack of updates may make them risky to use.
This isn't the first time Nokia has been shifted out of the phone market. Microsoft purchased Nokia's mobile business after striking a deal in 2013. Then in 2016, the mobile phone company HMD (which was founded in part by former members of Nokia leadership) acquired the license to the Nokia brand for a new lineup of mobile phones. These phones were designed and manufactured by HMD, but bore the familiar Nokia logo. Now, HMD is phasing out the Nokia name in favor of its own HMD-branded smartphone lineup.
HMD isn't who owns Nokia, but it is the company responsible for utilizing the Nokia brand in the mobile device market. These days, Nokia's main business is in telecommunication, networking, and connectivity technology. But for consumers who associate the Nokia name with mobile phone products, the end-of-life of the G310 5G and XR21 smartphones represents the end of an era.
If you're still using one of the Nokia phones that are about to lose software support once and for all, it's probably time to start shopping around for a new alternative. The most natural replacement would be a newer HMD phone, but unfortunately, HMD unceremoniously exited the U.S. market in 2025.
If you're in the United States, your choices are limited to brands that can thrive in a competitive market. Owners say that the most reliable Android phones are typically the ones from Samsung and Google. The Samsung Galaxy S26 and Google Pixel 11 are both flagship releases from 2026 that won't steer you wrong if you can get past the sticker price.
Of course, the appeal of Nokia and HMD-branded smartphones has often been their balance of quality and affordability. If you're looking for budget phones that are worth buying in 2026, consider options like the Motorola Moto G, or a midrange Samsung model like the Galaxy A57 5G. These will provide a similar level of quality to what you may be accustomed to if you've been using the Nokia G310 5G or Nokia XR21 phones that are about to lose support soon.

73,2026-08-21,"
Privacy is shifting from a settings toggle to a core selling point across four software categories: browsers (Brave, DuckDuckGo, Firefox blocking trackers by default), messaging apps (WhatsApp view-once, Signal growth), VPNs (independent no-logs audits, RAM-only servers), and AI tools (on-device processing, client-side encryption). The common thread: users now demand verifiable proof, not promises.
For the longest time, the tech industry’s unofficial motto was “if the product is free, you are the product.” Most of us just accepted it. But after a decade of data scandals, tracking revelations, and privacy blunders, that logic is starting to feel dated.
We’ve hit a tipping point. A quiet but powerful shift is happening across the software landscape, and in 2026, privacy is no longer a dusty toggle buried in the settings menu. It’s becoming the main event, a core selling point that companies are building their reputations on.
So where is this privacy-first shift most obvious? These are the software categories where the change is easiest to spot, and the clearest signs that your data is finally getting a bit more respect.
Finally, a private window that’s actually more private.
Let’s be honest: everyone knew “Incognito mode” was limited. It never stopped your ISP, your employer, or the websites you visited from seeing what you were up to. Users are demanding more now, and browser makers are listening.
You’re seeing a surge in browsers like Brave and the DuckDuckGo browser, which block ads and trackers by default. Even the big players are changing course. Mozilla has kept expanding Firefox’s privacy tools, and the industry is exploring standards such as Private Access Tokens to verify legitimate users with less tracking. That’s a real step up from a feature that mostly hid your search history from whoever else used the same laptop.
Your chats are increasingly for your eyes only.
Remember when private messages could be scanned to sharpen ad targeting? Creepy, right? The backlash against that model came fast, and it fueled the growth of private platforms like Signal.
That pressure has pushed bigger platforms to adapt. WhatsApp has rolled out stronger privacy controls, including view-once messages, while continuing to lean on end-to-end encryption. With governments still debating whether companies should scan private messages, secure communication has gone from a nice-to-have to a necessity.
Putting the “private” back in Virtual Private Network.
The VPN industry has long had a trust problem. For every strong service, there are plenty of questionable ones with vague “no-logs” claims that fall apart under scrutiny. Reports of VPNs handing user data to police and free apps leaking user data have left people understandably skeptical.
So how do you fix a trust problem? You stop saying and start proving. A new gold standard is emerging around verifiable evidence, and it’s pushing the market to clean up its act.
Top-tier providers are moving toward verifiable proof to regain user trust. Independent no-logs audits, such as those conducted for providers like IPVanish and ExpressVPN, are becoming mandatory baselines. At the same time, major services are actively transitioning to RAM-only server networks, where operational data is automatically erased upon every reboot rather than stored on persistent hard drives. Combined with regular transparency reports, this mix of third-party auditing and ephemeral infrastructure is becoming the benchmark users check when weighing up a secure VPN for privacy.
Smart assistants that don’t need your full life story.
With AI exploding, the next privacy battleground is already here. Can you get smart, personalized AI without it hoovering up every scrap of your personal data? A new wave of tools suggests the answer might be yes.
There’s a growing push toward on-device processing, where AI models run locally on your phone or laptop, as seen in Apple’s recent OS updates. Private AI platforms like Jatter use client-side encryption to offer personalized responses without storing conversation histories on remote servers. It’s still early, but it’s a promising sign that privacy-focused thinking is spreading to the next generation of software.
The overarching message is clear: privacy has evolved from a regulatory hurdle into a core product strategy and a primary competitive advantage. The software platforms built on unchecked data collection are now forced to compete on verifiable trust and structural transparency. In 2026, protecting user data is no longer just ethical, it’s good business.

74,2026-08-21,"The Dutch data protection authority has decided to fine Uber 825 million euros for deactivating driver accounts by software without telling the drivers properly. Only Meta's 1.2 billion euro penalty has been larger under GDPR. Uber will appeal.
Uber sign on its headquarters building in San Francisco, California, USA – June 6, 2023. Uber Technologies is a transportation conglomerate.
The Dutch data protection authority has decided to fine Uber €825mn, or about $966mn, for shutting down driver accounts by automated systems without telling the drivers properly.
It is the second-largest penalty ever issued under the General Data Protection Regulation. Reuters reported the decision after reviewing a document dated 17 August. The regulator, known as the AP, confirmed the decision and did not comment further.
Uber said it will appeal.
GDPR bars decisions taken by algorithm alone where they significantly affect someone. Those decisions require meaningful human involvement and a route for the person to challenge them.
For a driver, losing account access means losing the ability to work. That is the significant effect the rule is written around, and it is why the regulator treated a suspension as more than an account status change.
“The AP has determined that Uber violated drivers’ rights, specifically the right not to be subject to automated decision-making that has significant consequences,” the decision said.
It added that “Uber has also violated the right to be informed”. The regulator called the matter serious enough to warrant the size of the fine.
Uber temporarily suspended accounts of drivers its systems flagged for suspected fraud.
Two examples appear in the reporting. The systems concluded that some drivers had taken unnecessary detours to inflate fares. The software flagged others for accepting trips they did not intend to complete.
Uber said those suspensions were usually brief, and that it did not permanently deactivate accounts without human review.
The AP said Uber’s software sometimes removed drivers with low customer ratings permanently. Uber disputed that, saying it has never automated a permanent deactivation.
“We strongly disagree with this decision and disproportionate fine,” a spokesperson said.
The company said it takes drivers’ rights seriously and that its current policies include human review and a way for drivers to contest suspensions. It told DutchNews that it no longer permanently deactivates accounts through automated systems alone.
Uber gave a number to support its argument that the penalty is out of proportion. It said low customer ratings cost 126 drivers their accounts across Europe in 2021.
The AP has not published a figure for how many drivers the automated suspensions touched in total.
The decision covers incidents in Europe between 2020 and 2022.
It began with a complaint from drivers in France. Uber had suspended them on suspicion of fraud. The Dutch regulator took the case because Uber’s European headquarters sit in Amsterdam, which makes the AP the lead authority for Uber across the EU.
The AP has penalised the company repeatedly, and each fine has been larger than the last.
It fined Uber €600,000 in 2018. It issued a €10mn fine in early 2024 over drivers’ privacy rights. It then imposed €290mn over transfers of driver data to the United States, which had been its record and which Uber is also appealing.
That €290mn case grew out of the same French complaints as this one. The new decision is close to three times the size of it.
Ireland’s regulator fined Meta €1.2bn in 2023 for unlawfully transferring European Facebook users’ data to the United States. Meta is appealing.
Both of the two largest GDPR penalties on record therefore sit with regulators in small member states that host the European headquarters of large American companies.
Reuters noted that appeals running for years often shrink or overturn headline fines against large technology companies.
The desk has covered how long that takes. Google lost its final appeal over a €4.1bn Android penalty in July, years after the original decision. Google separately took an €890mn fine, the first issued under the Digital Markets Act.
European regulators have issued billions of euros in penalties against American technology companies under privacy, competition and digital market rules. Regulators have fined Meta, Google, Apple and Amazon more than once each.
US President Donald Trump has criticised the practice. In April, a senior US State Department official called the fines the “biggest single source of friction” in economic relations between the United States and the EU.
The provision Uber was found to have breached is the part of GDPR that governs decisions made about people by software.
The question of what software may decide about a worker has come up elsewhere. TNW reported this month on an AI store manager that fired an employee. It needed a reminder of its own rules first.
Uber’s drivers have also been the subject of data questions outside Europe. The desk reported in August that a surveillance company pitched turning Uber and Lyft drivers into a roaming camera network.
The Dutch regulator has been assertive on data matters more broadly. The Netherlands blocked an American company from buying the cloud provider that runs Dutch digital identity in May.
Uber will appeal, and it has not said when it will file. Dutch appeals of this kind go first to the courts rather than back to the regulator.
The AP confirmed the decision but did not comment beyond that. Neither the regulator nor the company has published the full text.
The two sides do not agree on the facts. The AP says low-rated drivers sometimes lost their accounts to software alone. Uber says that never happened. That disagreement is what an appeal would test.
Uber also has not said whether the €825mn is provisioned in its accounts, and the AP has not set a date by which the company must pay.

Meta has launched Pocket across the United States. The app it is based on shut down the same day.
Pocket had been testing quietly in Brazil since July. It is now available to everyone in the US.
Pocket lets people type a prompt and get back a small interactive thing, which Meta calls a gizmo. Those gizmos publish to a scrollable feed where other people can play them, save them, repost them or remix them.
The company describes it on its own site as “a new creative platform to make and share gizmos”. It is free, on iOS and Android.
Pocket comes from the team behind Gizmo, an app built by Atma Sciences and founded by former Snapchat engineers.
Meta did not buy that company. It hired the team and took a non-exclusive licence to the technology, Business Insider reported, having scooped the move in March.
Business Insider, which has used both, describes Pocket and Gizmo as “pretty much identical app experiences”. Even the vocabulary carried over. Gizmo called its creations gizmos, and so does Pocket.
Atma announced on Thursday that it is winding down. “Earlier this year, our entire team joined Meta,” the company posted, “where we have been working on a new app.”
Josh Siegel, who led it, called the shutdown bittersweet, “a labor of love by so many people for the last couple years”. He said he was excited for Pocket.
Hire the team, licence the technology, leave the company standing until it is not.
TNW reported this month that Nvidia agreed to pay Poolside $6bn for a licence and jobs for 109 of its staff, in a deal both sides insisted was not an acquisition.
Meta’s version cost less and moved faster. The team joined in March, the product shipped in July, and the original was gone by August.
A licence is not an acquisition, and neither is a hiring round. The outcome here looks the same as one.
The creations respond to taps, swipes, dragging and the tilt of a phone.
They can play sound effects and clips of songs, and they can pull photos from a camera roll or use the camera and microphone directly.
Pocket has comments, likes and reposts. It has no direct messages. Links to individual gizmos open without the app installed, which is how Meta expects them to spread.
Creators can also fine-tune a prompt after the fact, adjust the visuals and add music, rather than starting again.
Meta told Business Insider there are no plans to connect Pocket to Instagram or its other apps at the moment. That is unusual for the company, which normally routes new products through the audience it already has.
Pocket is built on Muse Spark, the model from Meta Superintelligence Labs.
TNW reported in June that Meta had repeatedly delayed the Muse Spark API that outside developers were promised. The model launched in April, the interface slipped, and Meta had no scheduled date until it said it would arrive that month.
The desk’s line then was that a model without an API is a demo rather than a platform. Meta has now shipped its own consumer app on it.
The company has not said what the current API position is, and this piece does not claim to know. What is on the record is that Meta shipped a consumer app on the model before outside developers got a stable way in.
Pocket joins a run of standalone launches.
Instagram Instants came first, then Forum for Groups, Seller for Marketplace, an experiment in AI bedtime stories, a Creator Studio app, and this week a Meta AI app for Mac.
The company also runs Vibes, an earlier experiment in AI-generated video, inside its Meta AI app.
Zuckerberg told analysts in July that AI-assisted development is why. “I expect it to become a lot easier to ship new apps,” he said. “So we are planning to build out more ideas and use our recommendation systems to scale them.”
Meta AI already reaches users elsewhere in the family. It arrived in Threads DMs worldwide in July.
Vibe-coded apps and games have been filling app stores, which is part of Pocket’s pitch. A gizmo is not a download.
The money in the category is real. Emergent reached a $1.5bn valuation in July, and Lovable hit $500mn in revenue with 146 employees.
Those tools build software people use for work. Pocket builds things closer to a meme you can touch.
The bet is that the second category is bigger, because it needs no reason to exist beyond being fun.
Business Insider names Sekai and Wabi as startups attempting the same social version, and reports that venture investors are backing the category.
Meta has not said how many people used Pocket in Brazil during the July test. It has not said what it learned there, or why the US was next.
It has not given a figure for gizmos created, users, or any other measure of traction.
There are no ads in the app yet. Meta has not said whether there will be. Business Insider noted that a new app is a new advertising surface, and interactive posts are an obvious place to put a brand.
The terms of the Atma deal are not public. Neither Meta nor Atma has said what the non-exclusive licence cost, or exactly how many people moved across to Meta Superintelligence Labs.

76,2026-08-21,"YouTube Premium prices are rising across Europe, and a notice seen by TNW shows the company is asking subscribers to agree rather than simply charging them. Ignore the email and the subscription ends. Outside the EU, the same increase applies automatically after 30 days.
YouTube Premium is getting more expensive across Europe. This time the company is asking subscribers to agree, rather than simply imposing the increase.
A price-change notice seen by TNW went to a Romanian subscriber on Friday. It puts the individual plan up from RON 29.00 to RON 32.00 a month, a rise of about 10%. The notice carries a heading that reads “Action Needed”.
The wording underneath is the part worth reading twice. “To keep your Premium benefits, you will need to accept the new price,” it says. “If you take no action by October 10, 2026, your subscription will end before the new price takes effect.” Anyone who agrees sees the change on 12 October.
Doing nothing does not mean paying more. It means being cancelled.
The notice sets out what the money buys before it names the number. Ad-free and downloadable videos, background play, and access to more than 300 million tracks on YouTube Music. The increase will “allow us to continue to improve Premium and support the creators and artists you watch”, it says. YouTube also raised the price of YouTube Music Premium recently, so the pressure is not confined to one product.
That is not how the same rise is landing outside the European Union.
In Singapore, the individual plan goes from S$13.98 to S$15.98 and the family plan from S$27.98 to S$31.98, according to Digital Trends, citing the Straits Times. New subscribers already pay the higher rate. Existing members get the new price automatically after at least 30 days. Nobody has to click anything.
The American increase in April went further still. YouTube moved the US individual plan from $13.99 to $15.99, and Digital Trends headlined its coverage at the time on the fact that nobody got a heads-up.
EU consumer law is the obvious difference, though YouTube has not said so. Several member states require a trader to get express consent for a price change. The bloc’s unfair terms rules go further. They treat a clause allowing unilateral price rises as void, unless the consumer can see the reason and walk away. Silence, under that framework, is not agreement. An opt-in flow with a hard deadline is one way to satisfy it.
The rises do not share a percentage, which makes the rollout hard to summarise.
Romania is up about 10%. Singapore and Spain are both up around 14%, on figures from the Straits Times and a subscriber screenshot circulated on X. Android Authority reports a steeper move in Portugal and Italy, where the individual plan goes from €17.99 to €20.99. That is closer to 17%. Finnish subscribers report going from €14.99 to €16.99.
Those last figures come from posts on Reddit and ResetEra. Android Authority was careful to say the reports were not yet official when it published on Friday morning. The notice TNW has seen is an official one. Google sent it as what the company calls a mandatory email service announcement.
Android Authority also notes that European Premium prices had been unchanged since 2024. A rise arriving four months after the American one therefore reads as a catch-up, not a synchronised global move.
The increase lands in the middle of a run of changes that all point the same way.
In July, YouTube said Premium would include Peacock at no extra cost from early 2027, in what was Peacock’s largest distribution deal. A month later the base price is going up. The two things are not in contradiction, but they are the same subscription.
Creators are being squeezed from the other end. YouTube doubled the entry requirements for its Partner Program earlier this month. From 24 August it also counts a view from the first frame, which will make view counts rise without moving earnings.
Subscribers pay more, the bar for getting paid goes up, and the headline numbers on the platform inflate. Each change has its own justification. Together they describe a business collecting more at both ends.
European authorities have already taken an interest in how big subscription price rises get communicated.
Italy’s competition authority opened an antitrust probe into Microsoft 365’s AI-driven price rise in June. The UK’s Competition and Markets Authority began examining in July whether Microsoft misled customers over the same thing.
Neither concerns YouTube, and neither turned on the mechanics of consent. Both establish something else. A large platform raising subscription prices in Europe now does it with regulators watching the disclosure as closely as the number.
On that measure the notice is careful work. It states the old price, the new price, the deadline, the billing date, and what happens if the subscriber ignores it. That is more than many price rises tell you.
The practical position is simpler than the rollout suggests.
Anyone in an affected YouTube Premium market gets an email. That email is currently the only reliable indication of what they will pay. YouTube has published no list of countries and no schedule. Neither Digital Trends nor Android Authority could establish the full scope, because there is nothing central to check.
Android Authority reports that some subscribers have cancelled since the emails went out. Anyone weighing that has a middle option, because YouTube sells a cheaper Premium Lite tier that strips out most of the music benefits.
The deadline matters more than the price. A subscriber who misses the notice, or leaves it in a promotions folder, does not drift onto a higher tariff. They lose the service. In the Romanian case they lose it before the new price would even have applied.
YouTube has not said which markets are next, or whether the American plan rises again. The notice settles three things. The European round is real, it has dates attached, and this time the company is asking.
